# Agreements, from scratch: history

13 November 2026. A record of work by Claude. Not a decision, and not part of the grammar. Nothing here is read by a verifier.

**The wish** (Nobody, allegedly, `docs/decisions.md`, branch `claude/lead-records-2`): "I've decided for a total rewrite of agreements from scratch." "No translation, fresh from the research we had all agreed was excellent direction." On what must hold: "can I with a single click reward all the people that contributed to the service or good via MOR". And: "Building blocks over solutions."

**What this file is.** From draft 10, `draft-10.md` holds the grammar alone, readable by a stranger with no history. That is the MOR lead's decision, as housekeeping. Everything about how the text came to be is here:

- **Part A**: how draft 9 came to be. The opening paragraphs of `draft-9.md` and its table "Changes, and the words each comes from", unchanged.
- **Part B**: sections 9.1 to 9.15 of `draft-9.md`, unchanged: what the tests found, the lists left open by each draft for Nobody, allegedly, where the ledger's arrived-at lines say more than the exchanges, and the list "Building blocks over solutions".
- **Part C**: draft 10. One table for its changes, where each change landed, each place a word changed, and where the ledger's lines say more than the two exchanges it was written from.
- **Part D**: draft 11. One table for its changes, what the click test showed and which change answers it, where the ledger's lines and the MOR lead's proposal say more than the three exchanges it was written from, and draft 10's list of open items, unchanged. From draft 11, `draft-11.md` holds the grammar alone; `draft-10.md` stays in the repository.
- **Part E**: draft 12. One table for its changes, where each landed, where the ledger's lines say more than the three exchanges it was written from, and draft 11's list of open items, unchanged. From draft 12, `draft-12.md` holds the grammar alone; `draft-11.md` stays in the repository.
- **Part F**: draft 13. One table for its changes, what the second click test showed and which change answers it, where each change landed, where the ledger's lines say more than the three exchanges it was written from, and draft 12's list of open items, unchanged. From draft 13, `draft-13.md` holds the grammar alone; `draft-12.md` stays in the repository.
- **Part G**: draft 14. One table for its changes, where each landed, where the ledger's lines and the MOR lead's proposal say more than the one exchange it was written from, and draft 13's list of open items, unchanged. From draft 14, `draft-14.md` holds the grammar alone; `draft-13.md` stays in the repository.

One table for its changes, every word that was renamed, where the ledger's lines and the brief say more than the three exchanges it was written from, where each change landed, and draft 14's list of open items, unchanged. From draft 15, `draft-15.md` holds the rules alone, and each rule's plain words and reasoning are in `reasons-15.md`; `draft-14.md` stays in the repository.

**How to read Parts A and B.** They are draft 9's words, moved and not edited. "This draft" and "this text" there mean draft 9. Section numbers 0 to 8 there are draft 9's sections; sections 9.1 to 9.15 are Part B of this file. Rule and clause numbers there are draft 9's: Part C says which of them draft 10 changed (rule 11 is another rule in draft 10; rules 1.6 to 1.9, 10.7 and 10.9 are gone; draft 9's rule 10.8 is draft 10's rule 10.7). "Payment chain", "the baker's chain" and "arbiter" there are draft 9's words; draft 10 says "sequence", and has no arbiter. The notes inside each rule that say which draft added which clause, and the draft-by-draft reasoning under each rule, are not moved here: they stay where they are, in `draft-9.md`, which remains in the repository as the full record.

# Part A. How draft 9 came to be (from `draft-9.md`, unchanged)

**Read for this draft, and nothing else:** `docs/agreements-from-scratch/draft-8.md` and `companion-3.md` (branch `claude/agreements-draft-8`, pull request 160), and `docs/decisions.md` (branch `claude/lead-records-2`). Draft 9 is draft 8 with what the ledger added under "13 November" after draft 8 was written brought in, and what in draft 8 depends on it brought in line. Nothing else changes. Four things were added there: in **Withdrawing an assent**, the exchange on "a counter signature" (S37); in **Grants**, the exchange on a condition amounting to "never" (S38); in **What void changes**, his direct answer (S39) and the MOR lead's wording that follows it (W2); and his principle, "Building blocks over solutions." (B). What draft 8 says about its own reading is kept as draft 8's record. Draft 8 was draft 7 (read from `draft-7.md` and `companion-2.md`, branch `claude/agreements-draft-7-dvg4lj`, pull request 159) with the exchanges the ledger then recorded under "13 November" brought in. There were ten entries there: the receiver and the service agreement (S28), which version a discharge divides by (S29), whose anchoring wins (S30), the work's side of its deal (S31), what void changes (S32), grants (S33), withdrawing an assent (S34), a lone sale in another agreement (S35), an offer that closes by itself (S36), and the MOR lead's own settlements of wording and mechanics (L). With them came Q2, the typos he corrected on 13 November. Draft 7 was draft 6 with six changes (S23 to S27, and W); draft 6 was draft 5 with three changes (S21 and S22); draft 5 was draft 4 with three changes (S18 to S20) and Q; draft 4 was draft 3 with three changes (S15 to S17); draft 3 was draft 2 with rule 11 rewritten (S12 to S14); draft 2 was written from `docs/strangers-2026-11/` (README, `agreement-tasks.md`, `agreement-grammar.md`, `late-roles.md`), `draft-1.md`, and the test results `situations-1.md` and `shower-cmips-1.md`; test 2 is `shower-cmips-2.md` (branch `claude/shower-cmips-2-k9cesp`, pull request 154). None of those was reopened for draft 9: where this text quotes them, it quotes draft 8. How the research is used is recorded in `companion-3.md`, written for draft 8, where it replaced `companion-2.md` (L); draft 9 changes two of its rows (#6 and #48) and nothing else in it.

**Rule numbers.** In plain words: the central rule, "an agreement as a party", was the tenth rule in drafts 2 to 7. It is now the first, and the nine rules that stood before it each move down by one. Precisely (from L, the MOR lead's settlement: "rule 10, the central idea, is renumbered first"):

| Drafts 2 to 7 | Draft 8 | Name |
|---|---|---|
| rule 10 | rule 1 | An agreement as a party |
| rule 1 | rule 2 | Whole |
| rule 2 | rule 3 | Author |
| rule 3 | rule 4 | In force |
| rule 4 | rule 5 | Own signature |
| rule 5 | rule 6 | Own chain |
| rule 6 | rule 7 | Place to look |
| rule 7 | rule 8 | Say who said it |
| rule 8 | rule 9 | Unchecked is not passed |
| rule 9 | rule 10 | Spots |
| rules 11, 12, 13 | unchanged | Seen, by the agreed witness; Forks; Now is what an act cites |
| (none) | rule 14 | The baker (new, from S35) |

Clauses keep their place inside their rule: what was rule 3.8 is rule 4.8, what was rule 10.4 is rule 1.4. **Every rule number in this text is draft 8's**, also in the rows and items written for earlier drafts, and where they say what an earlier draft added ("draft 5: new rule 4.8" was rule 3.8 when draft 5 was written). Nothing else in those rows and items was changed by the renumbering. Section numbers of this text, task numbers (K), parameters (P), test findings (F, M, N, E) and the numbers inside his quoted words are not rule numbers and did not move. Draft 9 moves no number: its rule numbers are draft 8's.

**How the exchanges are read.** Each exchange in the ledger records the MOR lead's question or proposal, the words of Nobody, allegedly, quoted exactly, and a line "Arrived at (to check)" written by the lead. Nobody, allegedly, on how to read them: "My words matter less than our words within that conversation do", and "Be mindful of taking isolated sentences out of context and give them too much weight". So each exchange is read here as a whole: the lead's question and his answer together. The arrived-at line is the lead's reading, not his words. Where that line says more than the exchange supports, this draft follows the exchange, and section 9.14 lists the difference (sections 9.10 and 9.12 are the lists of drafts 7 and 8, kept). Where an exchange does not cover something a change touches, the text stays as draft 8 has it, and section 9.13 lists it (section 9.11 is draft 8's list, kept). The last entry under "13 November" is different in kind: it is the MOR lead's own settlement, under the delegation Nobody, allegedly, gave for technical wording and mechanics, and the ledger records it as the lead's. It is row L below, and is marked as the lead's wherever it is used. So is the wording that follows his answer in "What void changes": row W2.

**The word "void".** In plain words: from draft 9 the grammar has no term "void" (S39, W2). The word is gone from the definitions, the fields, the rules, the tasks and the costs. It is still written in three kinds of place, and nowhere else: in his own quoted words; in the rows of the table below and the items of section 9 that were written for drafts 7 and 8, which keep the words of their draft as its record (as rows S19 and S21 kept "resolve" and "resolver" after W); and where this text says what draft 7 or draft 8 called something. Where those rows and items say "void", draft 9 says "no longer in force, by its own condition"; where they name the Terms field `void`, draft 9 names `condition`; and where they say "withdrawal", draft 9 says "counter signature" (S37).

"Test 2" below means `shower-cmips-2.md`; its findings are N1 to N7, and it re-read test 1's M1 to M5.

Task numbers with a hash, such as (#23 Hold), refer to the research's 49 tasks in `agreement-tasks.md`. Task numbers with a K, such as K4, are this text's tasks (section 5).

**Every quotation of his words** in this draft is taken from `docs/decisions.md` as it now stands, including the typos he corrected there on 12 November (Q below) and on 13 November (Q2 below), and the two the ledger records as corrected with him in the words S21 quotes ("really", from "cruelly"; "were", from "they'd were"), the two in the words S23 quotes ("addresses" and "service"), and the two in the words S34 and S35 quote ("until" and "baker"). The four words draft 7 quoted uncorrected (its section 9.9, item 17) are the four he corrected on 13 November, and are quoted here corrected. Where a quotation runs over several lines there, it is written here on one line. How the research's 49 tasks were used is now in `companion-3.md`. The words S37 to S39 quote are given as the ledger has them; three of them may be typos, and are left as they stand until he says (section 9.13, item 13).

## Changes, and the words each comes from

Only the statements of Nobody, allegedly, below change the drafts. Each is quoted as the brief for its draft gives it. S1 to S11 changed draft 1 into draft 2; everything else in draft 1 was kept. S12 to S14 changed draft 2 into draft 3: they are about draft 2's rule 11, and only rule 11 and what depends on it changed. S15 to S17 change draft 3 into draft 4. Each of them answers a question put by the MOR lead after test 2. S18 to S20, and Q, change draft 4 into draft 5: S18 to S20 answer the MOR lead's questions on forks, and Q is his correction of typos in his own quotes. S21 and S22 change draft 5 into draft 6: they answer the MOR lead's questions on ending an agreement and on withdrawing an open offer, and are read together, as one train of thought, a later answer refining an earlier one; no single sentence of them is made a rule of its own. S23 to S27, and W, change draft 6 into draft 7. Each is one exchange from the ledger, named by the title the ledger gives it and read as a whole. W is different in kind: its wording is the MOR lead's, chosen under the delegation Nobody, allegedly, gave for technical wording, and is recorded as the lead's. The six changes of draft 7, numbered as its brief numbers them, are: **1**, "agreement as a party" first (S24, with S23); **2**, the payment chain (S23); **3**, an offer whose author is gone (S25); **4**, rights as the version offers (S26); **5**, "now" is what an act cites (S27); **6**, words (W). S28 to S36, Q2 and L change draft 7 into draft 8. S28 to S36 are each one exchange from the ledger's "13 November", named by the title the ledger gives it and read as a whole. Q2 is his correction of typos. L is the MOR lead's own settlement, recorded as the lead's. The ten changes of draft 8, numbered as its brief numbers them, are: **1**, the receiver may be an identity or an agreement (S28); **2**, a payment is divided by the version it was made under (S29); **3**, the clock and place an agreement names bind every reader of it (S30); **4**, a deal states its own fee and how late payments are handled (S31); **5**, void is as the version states (S32); **6**, delegation (S33); **7**, nothing counts until every party has signed, and an assent may be withdrawn before then (S34); **8**, the baker (S35); **9**, an offer may close by its own signed condition (S36); **10**, the MOR lead's settlements (L). S37 to S39, W2 and B change draft 8 into draft 9. S37 to S39 are each the later part of an exchange of the ledger's "13 November", added there after draft 8 was written; each is read together with the earlier part of the same exchange (S34, S33, S32). W2 is the MOR lead's wording, recorded as the lead's. B is a principle of his. The four changes of draft 9, numbered as its brief numbers them, are: **1**, a counter signature (S37); **2**, conditions on ending a delegation (S38); **3**, no term "void" (S39, W2); **4**, "Building blocks over solutions" as the test for every change (B). The questions are the lead's, given here only so that the answers can be read, and only his answers decide. Where a test result (`situations-1.md`, `shower-cmips-1.md`, `shower-cmips-2.md`) found a gap these words do not cover, it is not closed: section 9 lists it.

| | His words | What changed (Claude's reading) |
|---|---|---|
| S1 | "found it strange that “task” is not defined." | **Task** and **cMIP** are defined (section 2). The research's 49 tasks and this text's tasks are told apart. |
| S2 | "Isn’t “how the 49 tasks land” more of a companion section, it says: this is how the research was used." | The landing table left section 5 and is now the companion (`companion-3.md` from draft 8), with how `late-roles.md` was used. |
| S3 | "It’s not ruling… it's setting up grammar for people to decide how to agree on rules." | Sections 0 and 1 say Agreements is a grammar. The eight rules of draft 1 are named **grammar rules**: they say how acts are read, never what people must agree. People's own rules are cMIPs. |
| S4 | "Just be mindful to not attempt to solve something the cMIPs will." | Draft 1's "design direction for cMIP authors" left this text (now in the companion). Every new rule below says only what a verifier reads; how a thing is proven is left to a cMIP. |
| S5 | On a party being a work (another agreement), the money going to whoever that agreement names as its receiver now: "I think it is essential… it ensures a series of split is possible." And: "The new agreement points to the other agreement as one party, no collective involved". | A party may be an **agreement** (grammar rule 1). Terms may name a **receiver**. What is owed to an agreement goes to its receiver now. |
| S6 | "some participants are not tied to a specific ID when the agreement is drafted, they are tied to a role that will be linked to an ID in the future." | A role is a **spot**, linked to a signer later (grammar rule 10). |
| S7 | "Prove that you have done X and earn spot 1. Prove you have received Y and earn spot 2. Prove that you are holding Z and earn spot 3 (relays for example) Prove that your ID fits the conditions in W and earn spot 4" | A spot has one of four kinds: **done**, **received**, **holding**, **fits** (grammar rule 10). New task K12 (Spot). |
| S8 | "Maybe a customizable “future participants” with the cMIP specifying how that participant is defined and confirmed", and "To be used only for" (the brief: those no agreement could anticipate), with "Mor won’t force the shower, it will sustain it". | A fifth kind, **future**, wholly defined and confirmed by its cMIP, for those the four kinds cannot anticipate (grammar rule 10.4). Nothing in the grammar makes anyone pay a lineage (section 0). |
| S9 | On roles rewarded for being seen: "seen by the agreed witness and with the possibility to reject all that is not tied to a settlement." | Draft 2: grammar rule 11, with an act kind **reject**, a Terms field `may_reject` and a Statement field `settlement`. Rule 11 is rewritten in draft 3 by S12 to S14; the witness is kept. |
| S10 | "Sadly some rails will need pools… this headache won’t be easy." | Pools are open parameter P6, said plainly. |
| S11 | "reputation tracking no platform owns or computes is important." | New task K13 (Standing): computed from signed acts by anyone, owned by no one. |
| S12 | On draft 2's rule 11: "Well, more than rejected is never setup to be counted. “Show me referees that lead to settlement only”". Then, on "referees": "Referees might have been a typo... if referrer is used does it makes more sense?", and, asked whether to record "referrers" as what he meant: "Yes pls." | Draft 3: "referees" is read as "referrers", as he confirmed. Nothing is rejected. What is counted is only what the agreement's own rule (task K4) sets up to be counted; anything else simply is not counted (rule 11.3). A rule may count only the counts that led to a settlement; the field `settlement` stays so that a rule can ask that (rule 11.4). |
| S13 | "Advertisement industry can still build a model that still rewards pure attention, if they wish." | Draft 3: the grammar does not require a count to be tied to a settlement. A rule may count pure attention, every count the witness states (rule 11.4). |
| S14 | "Yes, I never meant for a rejection to be something. It was in a cMIP driven scenario." | Draft 3: the act kind **reject** and the Terms field `may_reject` are removed. Whether counts are tied to settlements, and how, is left to cMIPs. |
| S15 | On old Terms stopping: "Old terms stopping is a headache I did not get to understand quite yet… that’s where a whole lot of special cases were born if I remember right". Asked (by the MOR lead) whether Terms stop being in force when Terms replacing them are in force (latest per the agreement's place, otherwise "as shown"), two competing replacements being a fork that is shown and never chosen: "Yes, less likely to bloat and overdefine." | Draft 4: one rule, with no list of cases (rules 4.5 to 4.7). Terms stop being in force when Terms replacing them are in force; which Terms are in force is answered per the agreement's place, otherwise as shown; two replacements of the same Terms both in force are a **fork**, shown and never chosen. Brought in line with it: the answer **fork** (section 2), rules 7.2 and 1.4 ("receiver now"), P10, a stated cost, and test findings F1 (in part), F4 and N1 (in part). |
| S16 | Asked (by the MOR lead) whether a spot is earned per proof, not once (each filling tied to the proof that earned it; the cMIP saying whether a spot holds one or many): "Yes, it leverages the above discussion as intended I think" | Draft 4: a spot may be filled many times, each filling tied to the proof that earned it; whether a spot holds one or many is its K12 cMIP's (rule 10.2). Brought in line with it: **fill** (section 2), rule 10.3, task K12, P7, and test findings F13 and N2. |
| S17 | Asked (by the MOR lead) where a relay stops "holding" and starts "being seen": "Relay was just an example. I don’t think it should be defined, what matters is “HOLD” with cMIP defined what the proof is, no?" | Draft 4: no use of holding is named in the grammar, as an example or otherwise; what proves holding is the K12 cMIP's. Taken out: the relay in rule 10's plain words and in whom it protects, and section 8's item on showing a holding. K12's trust for holding is now "as its cMIP says". Where holding stops and being seen starts is not defined (test finding N5). The example stays only in the companion. |
| S18 | "Can we attempt  to solve incompatible forks (a fork that should not exist) for all sorts of acts that need it?" Asked (by the MOR lead) whether one shape fits all acts (a fork is shown, never chosen; it ends only by a join that names every branch and says which continues, or merges them; the join is signed by whoever could sign the last act both branches share; what others relied on in a dropped branch stands, shown as disputed): "I think it does. Let’s focus on unresolved forks…" | Draft 5: one new grammar rule, rule 12 (Forks), for every act that can fork, with no list of cases. A **line** is acts each naming the one before; a **fork** is two acts that each count as the next after the same act. It is shown, never chosen; a **join** ends it (rule 12.3); what others relied on in a **dropped** branch stands, shown as **disputed** (rule 12.5). Brought in line with it: section 2 (line, fork, join, dropped, disputed), the fields `joins` and `continues` (section 3), rules 4.7 and 6.2 (which now point at rule 12), section 0, the cost on honest forks, and draft 4's section 9.6, item 3. |
| S19 | On unresolved forks, to the MOR lead's three ways out (a resolver named in advance; if none, each party carries on from the last Terms all shared; what happened stays visible): "Resolver makes sense, the problem potentially exists, so deciding in advance how to solve it makes sense, and this way does not force a single way to do it." | Draft 5: Terms may name a **resolver** in advance, by fingerprint: a cMIP answering a new task, K14 (Resolve). Its answer ends a fork as a join does (rule 12.4). How it resolves is its cMIP's: the grammar forces no single way. |
| S20 | On the MOR lead's scenario of a fork of Terms (2a signed by two of three parties, 2b by another two): "Both term 2a and 2b are invalid as they do not carry the required signatures that were in term 1." Asked whether, for a real fork (the Terms let fewer than all replace them, or the same people sign two replacements), the Terms both branches replaced stay in force until a join or a resolver settles it: "It does, and more sense than anyone ending up stuck in the first one" | Draft 5: Terms replace earlier Terms only if they carry the signatures the earlier Terms require (new rule 4.8); without them they replace nothing and fork nothing. While a real fork of Terms stands, the Terms both branches replaced stay in force, and everything resting on "Terms in force" (receiver now, spots, divisions) answers from them (rule 4.7, rewritten). Brought in line with it: rules 4.5, 10.2 and 1.4, the definition of **fork**, P9, P10, the cost on a fork of Terms, and test 2's N1. |
| Q | Not a decision on the grammar: his correction of typos in his own quotes, which `docs/decisions.md` records (the ledger's words, not his): *"Typos in his quotes are corrected only with him: on 12 November he corrected "decoifying" (defined), "pints" (points), "spit" (spot), "ment" (meant), "tat" (that), "it!s" (it's), "PERSONALY" (personally), "the the cMIP" (the cMIP), and confirmed "referees" as referrers."* | Draft 5: every quotation of his words is taken from `docs/decisions.md` as it now stands. Changed: the quotes in S3 ("it's"), S5 ("points"), S7 ("spot 2"), S8 ("the cMIP") and S14 ("meant"), and where rules 1 and 11 quote the same words. The other corrected words do not occur in this text. "Referees" stays as he wrote it, read as "referrers" (S12). |
| S21 | Asked (by the MOR lead) whether ending an agreement is a replacement by Terms that say "ended": "That agreement can be ended in multiple ways… what happens to the work and its shares draws different ends." Then: "Not sure still pondering. An agreement never ends, it can resolve. It’s a new version of the agreement with new conditions signed by all signers of previous version". Then: "« we agree to resolve the previous agreement » is really what happens. The agreement stays and is useful. Imagine they all agree to make the work public and free, then one person attempts to sell it again but individually, the previous agreement says « sorry can’t do this alone, the other two signatures are needed » so the cheater has to modify and bake again…" Asked (by the MOR lead) whether resolving needs everyone who signed the previous version even where the Terms let fewer change them: "Terms rule, because they were agreed by all signers" | Draft 6, read as one train of thought, the last answer settling who signs. **Change 1:** an agreement never ends; it **resolves**, by a new version (a replacing Terms act) with new conditions, carrying the signatures the version before it requires (new rule 4.9; rule 4.8 as draft 5 has it). What happens to the work and its shares is in the resolving version's conditions; the grammar lists no ways. **Change 2:** the last version keeps binding: it stays in force, and keeps protecting those in it, until a version carrying those signatures replaces it; nothing else stops Terms (rules 4.5, 4.9). Brought in line with it: section 0, the definitions **version** and **resolve**, rule 4 (plain words, lie, protects, reasoning), rule 5.4 and rule 5's reasoning, rule 7's "protects", P3 and section 8 (wording only), P10, the cost "No default way out", test findings F1 and F17, and draft 4's section 9.4, item 5(d), and section 9.6, item 2. |
| S22 | On withdrawing an open offer: "The offer does not end, it is simply not open to new IDs anymore. Whoever bought the rights should still be able to point to a valid agreement that says so" | Draft 6, **change 3:** an open offer is not withdrawn; it **closes** to new identities. A new version, under the Terms' own rule (rule 4.8, and S21's "Terms rule"), marks the spot `closed`: no signer who had not filled it fills it from then on, and every filling already earned stays a filling, its signer a party, in that version, so it has a valid agreement to point to (new rule 10.5). Brought in line with it: section 0, the definition of **spot**, the role's fields (section 3.1), rule 10 (plain words, lie, protects, reasoning), P10, the cost "Revoking an open offer" (now "Closing an open offer"), and test findings F9 and F17. |
| S23 | **The payment chain.** The MOR lead walked a fork in which a payer paid the receiver now of a branch later dropped, and proposed that a payment name the version it read. He asked: "Why can’t the agreement directly point to the split? Or did they choose not to?" and "The correct chain is the agreement with the split service rather than the service." Then: "So, there is a work agreement with shares that dictates a split. A pointer for the payment which can be an agreement with a service. The id involved each provide the pointer to their addresses for the various rails through their home. then, the agreement with services specifies the address of the payment to the split on the various rails as well as plug the IDs and the share from the work agreement and specify the fee structure for the service, flat or %" The lead read it back with the payer pointing at the service agreement. He corrected: "The work agreement points to the service agreement which provides the address on the rail, at discharge the split service uses the terms of the work agreement (shares and ids) as derived from the service agreement with the work. The service has a deal with a work which happened to be signed by 3 signatures of various weights." And: "A collective is an agreement, more than an ID, we gave it ID capabilities (keys) and then removed them. So, agreements as parties is nothing new"; "A collective is simply an agreement that decided to define many aspects of how it can govern itself"; "So all agreements are collectives if there are shares, whether loose or hyper defined" | Draft 7, **change 2:** the payment chain, written as what a verifier reads (rule 1.6 to 1.9). The payer pays the work. The work agreement points to its **service agreement** (new Terms field `service`), a deal between the service and the work as a party. The service agreement gives the address on each rail and the service's fee. At discharge the service divides by the work agreement's shares and identities; each owner's addresses come from that owner's home. How a service works is its cMIP's (task K8); no task is added. The `receiver` of draft 6 stays, for an agreement that points to no split (rule 1.3, 1.4). With S24, **change 1** (below). Brought in line with it: section 0, section 1, the definitions **work**, **service**, **service agreement**, **shares**, **rail**, **address**, **home**, **discharge** and **receiver**, the field `service` (section 3.1), rule 1 (all of it), task K4's inputs, P8, the costs on "now", on a work that cannot be paid and on a service as a hand, and test findings M4 (part), N7 and 8.1. |
| S24 | **The key concept.** "I’m really happy with the work done on this round. The key concept is « agreement as a party » then it’s what you build with it that changes" | Draft 7, **change 1:** the text opens with "agreement as a party" as its central idea (section 0, section 1), and rule 1 is renamed **An agreement as a party**. Works, services, collectives and their deals are what is built with it. There is no separate notion of a collective: read with S23's last three quotes, an agreement with shares defines as much or as little of its own governance as it chooses. In draft 7 the rule kept its old number, the tenth, so that every reference in drafts 2 to 6 still pointed where it did (section 9.4, item 22); in draft 8 it is rule 1 (L). |

The lead said a contest records paid, not delivered, but cannot give money back with nobody left to sign. He: "That’s something payment services can build on. We escrow the payment and you get the peace of mind in exchange of a fee. That’s how the deep web black market works" Then, on exit scams, after the lead listed escrow designs and an early alarm computed from signed acts: "And cannazone closed after DDOS without scamming anyone… both exist"; "Exit scams… maybe there is a way to solve them, MOR only offers ways to ring the alarm"; "Yes, but it all happens elsewhere. MOR just tracks receipts" | Draft 7, **change 3:** nothing new in the grammar. The lead's proposal (a closer, an end date) is not added. A buyer who paid and got nothing contests: a Statement, which stays on the record (section 3.2, task K11), and from which anyone may raise the alarm (task K13). Getting money back is what an escrow service is built for: a hand (task K8), under its service agreement (rule 1.6), for a fee. Without one it is a stated cost (section 7, "Paid, and the author gone"). Brought in line with it: that cost, test finding F17, and draft 6's section 9.8, item 5. |
| S26 | **A later version and a closed spot.** The MOR lead asked whether a later version could undo a closed spot or remove fillings already earned (an offer's author signing that earlier licences no longer count). Then, on whether that version can itself limit the rights: "That version could have conditions tied to time, creators statements… bla bla bla… core does not care" To the lead's reading (a filling has exactly the rights its version offers, conditions included; no later version changes them without the holder's own signature): "Exactly. So the agreement should have a say to give a verifier the ingredients to provide VOID as a reply, no?" He: "Yes, that distinction matters. Lapsed feels associated to time. No later version changes them without the holder's own signature (10.6). Reopening a closed spot is a new version and touches no earned filling (new rule 10.7). A version may name, in the new Terms field `void`, the rule (a cMIP) under which its rights end; a verifier then answers **void** for the filling: a state, with its cause shown beside it (new rule 10.8). An act that never counted is not void; "invalid" stays the word for that, and is not made an answer of this grammar (section 9.4, item 31). A delegation that only the agent may end is not "never": whether it will end is unknown, and the answer for it is **not shown**. Some conditions close an offer, some do not; that is the condition's. "Invalid" stays for an act that never counted."* | Draft 9, **change 3**, the wording, the lead's. Where draft 8 itself solves something a cMIP could build from the grammar's blocks, section 9.15 says so. Nothing is removed in this draft. That it is "the test for every change" is this draft's brief; the ledger records the principle and no more (section 9.14, item 5). |

Draft 1 had eight grammar rules and eleven tasks. Draft 2 has eleven grammar rules (three new, now numbered 10, 1 and 11) and thirteen tasks (two new: K12, K13). Draft 3 has the same eleven grammar rules and thirteen tasks, with one act kind fewer (reject) and one Terms field fewer (`may_reject`). Draft 4 has the same eleven grammar rules, thirteen tasks, acts and fields: rule 4 gains three clauses (4.5 to 4.7), rule 10.2 is rewritten, and task K12 produces fillings, each with its proof. Draft 5 has twelve grammar rules (one new: 12, Forks) and fourteen tasks (one new: K14, Resolve); Terms gain the fields `resolver`, `joins` and `continues`, and Statements `joins` and `continues`; rule 4 gains clause 4.8 and rule 4.7 is rewritten; answers gain the qualifier **disputed**. Draft 6 has the same twelve grammar rules and fourteen tasks: rule 4 gains clause 4.9 and rule 10 gains clause 10.5, and a role in `parties` may carry `closed`. Terms are never ended; only a version replacing them stops them. Draft 7 has thirteen grammar rules (one new: 13, Now is what an act cites) and the same fourteen tasks, K14 now named Arbiter. Terms gain the fields `service` and `void`, and `resolver` is renamed `arbiter`; Statements gain `relies_on`. Rule 10 gains clauses 10.6 to 10.8, rule 1 clauses 1.6 to 1.9, and rule 1.4 reads "now" as rule 13 says. Answers gain **void** and the qualifier **per anchoring**. Draft 8 has fourteen grammar rules (one new: 14, The baker) and the same fourteen tasks. The rule that was tenth is first, and the rules are renumbered (the table above). Terms lose the field `service` and gain `clock` and `envelope`; `receiver` may hold an agreement's root; a role in `parties` may carry `closes`; Statements gain the kind `withdrawal`. Rule 4 gains clause 4.10, rule 5 clause 5.5, rule 7 clause 7.4 and rule 10 clause 10.9; rules 1.3, 1.6 to 1.9, 10.8 and 13.4 are rewritten. Answers gain **invalid**. Draft 9 has the same fourteen grammar rules and fourteen tasks. The Statement kind `withdrawal` is named `counter_signature`, and the Terms field `void` is named `condition`. Rules 4.10 and 10.8 are rewritten, and rule 5.5 gains three sentences. Answers lose **void** and gain **no longer in force**.

# Part B. Sections 9.1 to 9.15 of `draft-9.md`, unchanged

## 9. For Nobody, allegedly: what the tests found that these words do not cover

Each item is a gap found by `situations-1.md` (F1 to F20), `shower-cmips-1.md` (M1 to M5, E8) or, new for draft 4, `shower-cmips-2.md` (N1 to N7, and its re-reading of M1 to M5). Sections 9.1 to 9.4 are draft 3's, with each item that S15, S16 or S17 answers marked **answered in draft 4** (F4 and F13 move to 9.1, since his words now cover them; F1 stays in 9.3, answered in part); nothing else in them changes. Each says what draft 2 did with it: **kept** (draft 1's text unchanged), **open** (written as an open parameter), or **cost** (written as a stated cost). Draft 3 kept every one as draft 2 had it; only the wording of M3's row changed, because it named rule 11. Section 9.5 is test 2's findings, each marked answered or left as draft 3 has it. Section 9.6 lists what draft 4's three changes leave for Nobody, allegedly. Draft 5 keeps all of this as draft 4 has it, marking only what S18 to S20 answer (**answered in draft 5**: F4 in 9.1, N1 in 9.5, item 3 in 9.6), adds its own readings to 9.4 (items 8 to 13), and lists in 9.7 what its changes leave for Nobody, allegedly. Draft 6 keeps all of this as draft 5 has it, marking only what S21 and S22 answer or touch (**answered in draft 6**, **touched in draft 6** or **not answered in draft 6**: F1, F9, F3 and F17 in 9.3, item 5(d) in 9.4, item 2 in 9.6), adds its own readings to 9.4 (items 14 to 21), and lists in 9.8 what its changes leave for Nobody, allegedly. Draft 7 keeps all of this as draft 6 has it, marking only what S23 to S27 and W answer or touch (**answered in draft 7** or **touched in draft 7**: F17 and M4 (part) in 9.3, items 3, 12 and 21 in 9.4, N1, N7 and 8.1 in 9.5, item 1 in 9.6, item 3 in 9.7, items 2, 5 and 8 in 9.8), adds its own readings to 9.4 (items 22 to 35), lists in 9.9 what its changes leave for Nobody, allegedly, and lists in 9.10 where the ledger's arrived-at lines say more than the exchanges. Items written for drafts 2 to 6 keep the words of their draft: where they say "resolve" or "resolver", draft 7 says "a new version" and "arbiter" (W). Draft 8 keeps all of this as draft 7 has it, with two changes to every item. First, rule numbers are draft 8's throughout (L; the table at the top): an item written when rule 4.8 was rule 3.8 now says rule 4.8. Second, it marks what S28 to S36 and L answer or touch (**answered in draft 8** or **touched in draft 8**: F3, F5, F6 and M5 in 9.3, items 3, 12, 22, 24 and 31 in 9.4, items 3, 4, 6 and 7 in 9.8, and most of 9.9). It adds its own readings to 9.4 (items 36 to 49), lists in 9.11 what its changes leave for Nobody, allegedly, and lists in 9.12 where the ledger's arrived-at lines for 13 November say more than the exchanges. Nothing was tested against draft 8. Draft 9 keeps all of this as draft 8 has it, marking only what S37 to S39 and W2 answer or touch (**answered in draft 9**, **touched in draft 9** or **in draft 9**: F3 in 9.3, items 43 and 46 in 9.4, items 6 to 9 in 9.11, items 4 and 6 in 9.12). Items written for drafts 7 and 8 keep the words of their draft, "void" and "withdrawal" among them. It adds its own readings to 9.4 (items 50 to 57), lists in 9.13 what its changes leave for Nobody, allegedly, lists in 9.14 where the ledger's lines say more than the exchanges, and lists in 9.15 where the text it inherits may solve what a cMIP could build (B). Nothing was tested against draft 9.

### 9.1 Covered by his words

- **M2. One agreement reading another**, and an amount owed to an agreement rather than a person. Covered by S5: rule 1. Test 2 found it closed for the receiver; with S15, also when the other agreement's Terms have been replaced (rule 1.4, rules 4.5 to 4.7).
- **F4. Two replacements of the same Terms can both be in force.** **Answered in draft 4** by S15: they are a fork, shown and never chosen (rule 4.7). **Answered in draft 5** by S20: they are a fork only if each carries the signatures the replaced Terms require (rule 4.8); while it stands, the replaced Terms stay in force (rule 4.7); it ends by a join or a resolver (rule 12).
- **F13. One agreement for all acceptors of an offer, or one each.** **Answered in draft 4** by S16 for the spot: a spot may be filled many times, each filling tied to its proof, and whether a second claimant fills the same spot is its K12 cMIP's (rule 10.2). Whether a filled spot passes on, or a later claimant displaces an earlier one, stays open (P7).

### 9.2 Touched by his words, not closed

- **F8. A newcomer not named in `parties`.** A newcomer foreseen in advance can now be a spot (rule 10; for a buyer of a part, kind received). A newcomer no one foresaw, admitted under a power, is as in draft 1: kept.
- **F14. How a successor becomes a party.** An heir can now be a spot of kind received (rule 10.3: a party in it from then on). Whether a successor named by a power acts `for` the one gone or in their own name is as in draft 1: kept.

### 9.3 Not covered

| Item | Gap | Draft 2 |
|---|---|---|
| F1 | Nothing makes Terms stop being in force: replacing, ending and revoking have no computed effect. | open: P10. **Answered in draft 4, in part**, by S15: replacing stops Terms once the replacing Terms are in force (rule 4.5). Ending without replacing, and revoking, stay open (P10). **Answered in draft 6** by S21: Terms are never ended or revoked except by a version replacing them; ending is resolving, a new version signed as the Terms require (rule 4.9). |
| F9 | An open offer with no power to end it can never be withdrawn, since "every party's own assent" includes a spot. | open: P10. **Answered in draft 6** by S22: an open offer is not withdrawn; a new version closes its spot to new identities (rule 10.5), signed as the Terms require (rule 4.8, S21: "Terms rule"). Read so (9.4, item 20), an unfilled spot is no party bound and signs nothing. |
| F3 | Withdrawal of an assent has no act any rule reads. | open: P10. **Not answered in draft 6**: his words are about an agreement and an open offer, not about an assent before Terms are in force (rule 4.9 is about Terms in force). Stays open (P10). **Answered in draft 8** by S34: a Statement of kind `withdrawal` takes back an assent to Terms not yet in force (rule 4.10). **In draft 9** (S37) that Statement is the counter signature, kind `counter_signature`. |
| F2, F2a | What "fork for that signer" removes; forking on purpose. | open: P9 |
| F5 | A double sale in two agreements is invisible to rules 6 and 7. | cost: "Ownership is as wide as its place". **Touched in draft 8** by S35: a stranger's sale of a baked work is seen (rule 14); one owner's double sale is still the cost. |
| F6 | A place gives positions, not dates. | open: P11. **Touched in draft 8** by S30: Terms may name a clock, which every reader uses (rule 7.4); what a clock is stays open (P16). |
| F7 | One act binding many people has `for`, which holds one person. | open: P12 |
| F10 | Rule 5.1(a) "they signed it in their own name" against "Stating Terms is not agreeing to them". | kept |

| F12 | Rule 5.4's "the Terms" when the grant and the deal name different places. | kept |

Whether death or loss of capacity changes that, and the pending assent, are as draft 5 (section 9.8, item 5). **Touched in draft 7** by S25, for the buyer of such an offer: nothing is added to the grammar; whoever paid and got nothing contests, on the record; getting money back is an escrow service's; without one it is a cost ("Paid, and the author gone"). The offer itself, and the pending assent, are as draft 6. |

| M5 | Money's answers (valid, invalid, pending, unknown) and these (shown, not shown, fork, unchecked) do not meet. | kept (section 8). **Touched in draft 8** by L: "invalid" is one word across the layers, and is an answer here (section 2); the rest is as before. |
