### 9.4 Readings of his words that he should confirm

These are Claude's readings, written into draft 2 (and, for item 2, rewritten in draft 3; items 5 to 7 are new in draft 4, items 8 to 13 in draft 5, items 14 to 21 in draft 6, items 22 to 35 in draft 7, items 36 to 49 in draft 8, and items 50 to 57 in draft 9) so that it can be read whole. Each could be wrong.

1. **S8, "To be used only for".** Read as: the future kind is for participants the four kinds of S7 cannot describe in advance (rule 10.4), with the gloss from the brief. Since no verifier can check that, rule 10.4 lays it on the authors.
2. **S12 to S14, what is counted.** Draft 2's reading of S9 (the Terms name who may reject, in `may_reject`) is withdrawn: his words say a rejection was never meant "to be something". Draft 3 reads them so:
   - (a) "never setup to be counted" is read as: the rule the Terms name decides which counts are counted, and nothing outside it is (rule 11.3). No act keeps a count out.
   - (b) "Show me referees that lead to settlement only" is read as a question a rule must be able to ask. So the Statement field `settlement` is kept, read by rule 11 and by K4 rules, rather than removed and left wholly to cMIPs. If he meant the tie to settlement to live only inside cMIPs, the field goes too.
   - (c) "referees" is read as "referrers". He said "Referees might have been a typo... if referrer is used does it makes more sense?", and, asked whether to record it so: "Yes pls." His words are quoted as he wrote them; only the reading changes. A referrer is a spot of kind done (rule 10), not a count of being seen. So his example says of referrers what rule 11 says of counts: of the referrers who fill a spot, only those the agreement's rule sets up to reward are rewarded, and a rule may reward only the referrers whose referral led to a settlement. Draft 3 already allows this without a new rule: `settlement` may be named by any Statement (section 3.2), and a K4 rule may read it. Rule 10 is not changed.
   - (d) The witness of S9 ("seen by the agreed witness") is kept (rules 11.1, 11.2), since S12 to S14 do not withdraw it.
3. **S5, the receiver.** His words say the money goes to "whoever that agreement names as its receiver now". Draft 2 reads that as a field `receiver` in Terms, read by the grammar, rather than a part of the body read by a cMIP. Whether a receiver may itself be an agreement is left open (P8a). **Draft 7:** the receiver stays, for an agreement that points to no service agreement (rules 1.3, 1.4; item 24). **Answered in draft 8** by S28: the receiver may be an agreement (item 36).
4. **S11, reputation.** Read as a task (K13) with no trust and no keeper, rather than a grammar rule: the grammar keeps what reputation reads (signed acts, proven forks, acknowledgments) checkable by anyone, and leaves the measure to cMIPs (S4).
5. **S15, replacing Terms.** His "Yes" answers the MOR lead's question as it was put. Draft 4 reads it so:
   - (a) "latest per the agreement's place" is read through rule 7.2 as it stands: a replacing Terms act counts once the place named by the Terms in force shows it. So an agreement that changes its place does so by Terms that must first be lodged at the old place.
   - (b) "never chosen" is read as holding per place too: a place's order does not pick the earlier of two replacements of the same Terms (rule 4.7, "by position or in any other way").
   - (c) The answer **fork**, which draft 3 gave only to one signer's two acts (rule 6), is also given to an agreement whose Terms branch, since his words call it "a fork that is shown and never chosen". The two forks are told apart by what they are about: a signer, or Terms.
   - (d) The rule is read as about replacing only. Ending Terms without replacing them, withdrawing an assent and withdrawing an open offer are not read into it (P10). **Answered in draft 6**, except withdrawing an assent: ending Terms is a version replacing them (rule 4.9, S21); withdrawing an open offer is closing its spot (rule 10.5, S22).
6. **S16, spots per proof.** A filling is read as a signer together with its proof, the acts the K12 cMIP rests on. A cMIP that says a spot holds one filling is read as also saying which, when two proofs come; whether a later one displaces an earlier one stays open (P7), because his words do not say.
7. **S17, holding.** Read as covering the whole grammar text, so the relay leaves rule 10's plain words, whom rule 10 protects, and section 8; it stays in S7's quoted words (his own) and in the companion. K12's confirmer for holding, which draft 3 gave as "the place or the holder", is read as part of what proves holding, so it becomes "as its cMIP says". The definition of **seen** (which includes "delivery to an audience") is not about holding and is unchanged.
8. **S18, who signs a join.** The MOR lead's words, which his "I think it does" answers, are "the join is signed by whoever could sign the last act both branches share". Draft 5 reads them as: whoever could sign, in that line, the next act after the shared act (rule 12.3). For one signer's line that is the signer; for Terms it is those whose signatures the shared Terms require for their replacement (rule 4.8). If he meant those who signed the shared act itself (for Terms, those who assented to it), the two readings differ wherever the Terms give a power to replace them to fewer than all.
9. **S18, the shape of a join.** The fields `joins` (the first act of every branch) and `continues` (the branch that goes on; absent, the join is the merge), and a join that names `continues` carrying no `parties`, `authors` or `body`, are Claude's way of writing "names every branch and says which continues, or merges them" as data. His words do not give a form.
10. **S19, where the resolver is named.** Read as: in Terms, by the field `resolver`, and the one that applies is named by the Terms in force where the fork stands (for a fork of Terms, the Terms both branches replace). Read as applying to every fork within the agreement, a signer's as well as its Terms', since S18 asks for one shape for all acts.
11. **S19, the other two ways out.** "Resolver makes sense" is read as answering the first of the MOR lead's three ways out. The second ("each party carries on from the last Terms all shared") is not made a rule of its own: for Terms, S20 says the same (rule 4.7); for one signer's line it has no meaning. The third ("what happened stays visible") is rule 12.2 and 12.5.
12. **S20, "invalid".** Read as: a Terms act without the signatures the Terms it replaces require replaces nothing and is not a branch (rule 4.8). No new answer "invalid" is added; such an act is simply not in force as a replacement. **Draft 7** keeps this reading beside the new answer **void** (item 31). **Answered in draft 8** by L: **invalid** is an answer, and is the one for such an act (rule 4.8; item 47).
13. **S18, "what others relied on".** Read narrowly, as an act by someone who signed none of the dropped branch's acts that names an act of it, or is made within or under its Terms (rule 12.5); "stands" is read as keeping the effect these grammar rules gave it, with **disputed** added to every answer resting on it.
14. **S21, one train of thought.** "That agreement can be ended in multiple ways" and "An agreement never ends, it can resolve" are read together, the later refining the earlier: the "multiple ways" are what a resolving version's conditions may say happens to the work and its shares, not ways the grammar lists. "Not sure still pondering" is kept with the words: rule 4.9 is the least they need, and the most this text reads into them.
15. **S21, who signs.** "signed by all signers of previous version" is read through his later answer, "Terms rule, because they were agreed by all signers": a resolving version carries the signatures the previous version requires for its replacement (rule 4.8), which are everyone's unless that version, agreed by all, gave a power to fewer.
16. **S21, resolving is replacing.** No field or act kind marks a version as resolving: any replacing version with new conditions resolves the one before, an amendment as much as what was once called an ending. "« we agree to resolve the previous agreement »" is read as what such a version's signers do, not as a word the grammar makes them write. If he meant a resolving version to be told apart from other versions by the grammar, a field would be needed.
17. **S21, his example.** "the previous agreement says « sorry can’t do this alone »" is read as the last version (the one that made the work public and free) still being in force, so that a lone sale binds no one else (rule 5) and a lone new version replaces nothing (rule 4.8). It is not read as a new rule on sales.
18. **S22, the closed spot.** "not open to new IDs anymore" is read as a field `closed` on the role, written by a new version (rule 10.5), rather than a new K12 cMIP or a Statement. "point to a valid agreement that says so" is read as: the closing version keeps the role and its earlier fillings, so the version in force says so; and the earlier versions stay readable (rule 4.9).
19. **S22, new IDs, not new fillings.** His words close the spot to new identities. A signer already in it who earns it again by a later proof (rule 10.2, S16) is not read as a new identity: whether they may is left to the spot's K12 cMIP, as in draft 5.
20. **S22 with S21, who signs a closing version.** Read by rule 4.8 as draft 5 has it ("every party bound by them", or what a power requires): an unfilled spot is no party bound, so it signs nothing; those who already filled it are bound by their own assents, so, unless the Terms give a power to fewer, they sign too.
21. **Resolve and resolver.** His word "resolve" (S21) is about an agreement; his word "Resolver" (S19) is about a fork. Draft 6 keeps both, told apart in section 2: resolving is a new version; a resolver is a cMIP that settles a fork. **Answered in draft 7** by W, in the MOR lead's wording: "resolve" is not a term, and the resolver is the arbiter (item 35).
22. **S24, where the idea stands.** "The key concept is « agreement as a party »" is read as asking that the text open with it (section 0, section 1) and name rule 1 for it. In draft 7 the rule was not moved to be the first in number: every reference in drafts 2 to 6, and in the tests, named it as the tenth. If he wants it first in number as well, the rules are renumbered. **Answered in draft 8** by L: it is rule 1, and the rules are renumbered.
23. **S23 and S24, no collective.** His three sentences on collectives are read as: the grammar defines none, and "shares" plus the powers of rule 5 are all the governance there is. "we gave it ID capabilities (keys) and then removed them" is read as history of earlier texts, with nothing to write here: an agreement named as a party has no key and signs nothing (rule 1.2).
24. **S23, the pointer.** "A pointer for the payment which can be an agreement with a service" is read as a Terms field, `service`, holding the root of the service agreement. "which can be", with "Or did they choose not to?", is read as a choice: so draft 6's `receiver` stays for those who do not point to a split. If he meant the service agreement to replace the receiver, rules 1.3 and 1.4 go (P8(c)). **Answered in draft 8** by S28, the other way round: the receiver stays and the field `service` goes.
25. **S23, who signs the deal.** "The service has a deal with a work which happened to be signed by 3 signatures of various weights" is read with "which" meaning the work: the work agreement is signed by its three owners, whose weights are its shares. So nobody signs the service agreement for the work, as rule 1.2 already says; the deal is shown by two pointers, the work's Terms naming the service agreement and the service agreement naming the work (rule 1.6). The ledger's arrived-at line reads the sentence the other way (section 9.10, item 1).
26. **S23, shares.** "a work agreement with shares that dictates a split" is read without a new field: shares are what the Terms say each party's part is, read by the K4 rule the Terms name, as dividing already was in draft 6. "plug the IDs and the share from the work agreement" is read as: the service agreement carries no copy of them; the service reads them in the work agreement (rule 1.9).
27. **S23, address, fee, discharge.** The address on each rail and the fee ("flat or %") are read from the service agreement's Terms in the form its cMIP gives, with no fields of their own. The service is read as a hand (task K8). "at discharge" is read as the moment the service pays on, and the grammar reads only the service's signed word of it (rule 1.9); whether "discharge" is the value layer's word is not known to this text (section 8).
28. **S23, homes.** "The id involved each provide the pointer to their addresses for the various rails through their home" is read as: an owner's address is the owner's word, published by their home, and is in neither agreement. "Home" is named and not defined.
29. **S26, "Whoever paid".** Read as whoever earned a filling, by paying or by any other proof (rule 10.2), since the lead's question was about fillings already earned. It is not read as a rule on other parties (P15).
30. **S26, the holder's own signature.** "No later version changes them without the holder's own signature" is the lead's sentence, which his "Exactly" answers. It is read as the holder's own assent (rule 5.1(a)), not an act made for them under a power. If a power the holder accepted in the version they earned under should also do, 10.6 says so in fewer words: that power is one of the version's own conditions.
31. **S26, void.** "the agreement should have a say to give a verifier the ingredients to provide VOID as a reply" is read as: a Terms field, `void`, naming a K4 rule, and a new verifier answer, **void**, for a filling, given with its cause. "Void to state" is read as: the filling stays a filling, in the state void, whatever ended its rights. "invalid" is not made an answer of this grammar: an act that never counted is **not shown**, as in draft 6 (item 12). If he wants **invalid** as an answer here too, it is one more answer in section 2. **Answered in draft 8** by L: it is.
32. **S26, the version a filling was earned under.** Read, with S27, as the version its proof names (rule 13.1), not the version in force when someone asks.
33. **S27, the form of rule 13.** His words are read into one new grammar rule instead of changes scattered over rules 7, 8 and 1. That every act names what it relied on (the lead's consequence, section 9.10, item 7) is written as a rule of reading (what is not named was not relied on), with a Statement field `relies_on` beside the fields that already name acts. "order emerging" is read as an answer a verifier may give from the acts alone, **shown** (13.3). "the anchoring they are willing to accept" is read as widening the word for where "nothing later" comes from, a place being one anchoring; rule 7 is not rewritten (P13).
34. **S25, the contest.** "that’s what the contest is for" is read as: a Statement of a kind a cMIP defines (task K11; the research's #41 Raise), by the one who paid and got nothing. No kind is defined in the grammar for it. "payment services can build on" is read as an escrow service being a service like the one that divides: a hand under a service agreement (rule 1.6). No rule is written for escrow.
35. **W, what is renamed.** The lead's wording names two things: no term for ending, and **arbiter** for the cMIP that ends a fork. Draft 7 also renames what follows from them: the field `resolver` to `arbiter`, and task K14 from Resolve to Arbiter. P3 is reworded ("replacing a grant"). The rows S19 and S21 in the table, and the items of section 9 written for drafts 5 and 6, keep their words as the record of those drafts.
36. **S28, one field.** "receiver still exists, it happen to also accept agreements hashes as input" is read as: one Terms field, `receiver`, holding one of the agreement's own parties or another agreement's root; draft 7's field `service` is removed. "hashes" is read as fingerprints, here roots. If he meant both fields to stay, `service` comes back and P8(c) reopens.
37. **S28, the deal and the public offer.** "the agreement with the split service, not some kind of public offer" is read as: the agreement named must itself name this work as a party, beside the service (rule 1.6, as draft 7 already asked). An offer open to everyone names no particular work, so the same two pointers tell it from the deal. Nothing new is checked.
38. **S28, automation.** "A split service can figure out a way to have agreements with customers automated on its end" is read as the service's own business, with nothing to write here.
39. **S29, the version a payment was made under.** "Whatever was signed (I accept your offer)" is read as the version the payment names (rule 13.2), not as requiring the payer to make an assent or fill a spot. "can only work if the signed deal stated so" is read as a condition stated by that same version, read where its cMIP puts it, with no field of its own. If he wants the grammar to read such a condition directly, it needs a field, as `void` has.
40. **S29, how far it reaches.** His words are read as about payments and their division only. "Only a signed condition can update the agreement on one end" may also answer draft 7's section 9.9, item 8 (the holder's own signature, or a power); draft 8 does not read it so (section 9.11, item 3).
41. **S30, the clock and the place.** His sentence names the clock; the place is in the lead's proposal that it answers. Both are written (rule 7.4). The clock is read as a Terms field, `clock`, beside `place`, and "the agreement states" as "the Terms in force name". "everyone" is read as every reader, party or not.
42. **S31, the deal states it.** Read as: the grammar gives no rule on who owes the fee or on late payments; a verifier reads the deal, and answers not shown where the deal is silent (rule 1.7). It is not read as requiring every deal to state them.
43. **S32, void.** Written as the lead's reading (section 9.12, item 4). Where the version states nothing on whether a void holder is still a party, or on leaving void, draft 8 adds no default: rules 10.3 and 10.8 read as in draft 7. **Answered in draft 9** by S39: nothing more is defined for it (item 54).
44. **S33, what a delegation is.** Read narrowly: Terms in which one delegator gives an agent a power to make acts for it. A power several parties share (a vote, a judge) is not read as a delegation each can end alone. "an agreement can add conditions to its ability to do so" is read as: the signed Terms of the delegation itself may state the conditions. "Its Terms' rule", for a delegator that is an agreement, is the lead's phrase; draft 8 says only "what that agreement's own Terms say" (P17).
45. **S34, "all parties".** "Nothing counts until all parties have signed" is read with the lead's proposal as: until the Terms are in force (rule 4.1). Where Terms carry their own `in_force` rule, that rule says which assents are needed, as before. If he meant that every party must always sign, whatever the Terms say, rule 4.1 changes and an agreement with a spot could never be in force.
46. **S34, the withdrawal.** "by a signed act" (the lead's line) is read as a second kind of Statement defined by the grammar, `withdrawal`, since rule 4 must read it. A signer who withdrew may assent again. **Answered in draft 9** by S37: the act is the counter signature (item 50).
47. **L, "invalid".** "one word across the layers" is read as: **invalid** is an answer of this grammar, meaning an act that never counted, and is used where draft 7 said such an act "replaces nothing" (rule 4.8), is "not void" (rule 10.8), and for Terms that are no agreement for a baked work (rule 14.3). The line between invalid and not shown is Claude's (P19).
48. **S35, the baker's rule.** "the agreement for that envelope" is read as Terms that name the envelope, in a new field, `envelope`. "one of the parties the agreement ... states" is read as a party bound by those Terms (their own assent, rule 5.1), since a name alone could be written by anyone. "through the chain of agreements of the work" is read as agreements for the same envelope, each naming the one before. "sold her bakers rights" is read without defining those rights: how they pass is each link's.
49. **S36, the closing condition.** "its own signed condition" is read as a rule (task K4) the role names in `closes`, written as `void` is; "open until June" reads the agreement's clock, "first 100 buyers" its place. **L, the companion and the numbers:** `companion-3.md` gives one landing per task, in draft 8's numbers, and drops the column that recorded draft 1's; rule numbers are changed everywhere in this text, old rows included, so that it can be read whole.
50. **S37, the name.** "a signature that cancels the previous one" is read as the act draft 8 called a withdrawal, and "the previous one" as the signer's own earlier assent to the same Terms, since the exchange is about withdrawing an assent. The Statement kind is named `counter_signature` for his words. If he would rather keep "withdrawal" for the act, only the name changes.
51. **S37, "once all parties have signed".** Read as draft 8 read "until all parties have signed" (item 45): until the Terms are in force (rule 4.1). So where Terms carry an `in_force` rule that asks for fewer than everyone, a counter signature stops being available when that rule is met (section 9.13, item 2).
52. **S37, an agreement as a party.** "all signature or whatever satisfies the collective agreement" is read as: "signed", for a party that is an agreement, is what that agreement's own Terms require. "the collective agreement" is read as that agreement itself, any agreement with shares being a collective in his words (S23). It is written only as a reading of "signed" in rule 4.10; rules 1.2 and 4.4 are not changed (section 9.13, item 1).
53. **S38, no limit, and "unknown".** His words answer one case, a grant only the agent may end; the lead's question put that case as "the one limit on conditions". Read together: no limit. It is written for conditions on ending a delegation only (rule 5.5). "I don.t know" is read as the answer **not shown** for whether such a delegation will end, and as a ban on answering "never". "Legible" is the lead's word, read as: the condition is in the Terms the delegator signed, and a verifier shows it.
54. **S39, the holder.** "The question depends on how things are setup" is read as: as the version states, which is what draft 8 wrote from the lead's reading; where the version says nothing, rule 10.3 reads as it did (a party from then on). "Void simply means, watever applied before this does not apply anymore" is read as covering the rights the condition is about, not the filling itself, which stays shown with its proof.
55. **S39, "updated again".** "Nothing stops the agreement to be updated again. So, offering again" is read, with the lead's line and this draft's brief, as a new version made as rule 4.8 says. "plug in values already in the chain (or not)" is read as: that version names earlier acts by fingerprint, and its own K12 cMIP says what they earn under it. Whether he also meant a return by condition, with no new version, is not read in (section 9.13, item 6).
56. **W2, the words as data.** The lead's wording is written as: an answer **no longer in force**, given "by its own condition" with the condition named; a Terms field `condition`, the name being Claude's; and "in force" defined for a filling and its rights as well as for Terms. "A right or a filling" is read as both together: the filling stays, and is answered by whether the rights it holds are in force. "Some conditions close an offer, some do not" is read against `condition` and `closes`, the two this text already has.
57. **B, the test.** "Building blocks over solutions" is read as a test for every change from now on, written in section 0 beside the two questions. Section 9.15 applies it backwards, as a list only. Nothing is removed on it.

### 9.5 Test 2 (`shower-cmips-2.md`): what it found, and what draft 4 does with it

Each finding is quoted or named as test 2 gives it. **Answered** means one of S15 to S17 covers it and draft 4 changes for it. **As draft 3** means his words do not cover it, and draft 4 leaves it exactly as draft 3 has it.

| Item | What test 2 found | Draft 4 |
|---|---|---|
| N1 | "\"Receiver now\" rests on \"Terms in force\", and nothing ends Terms": a replaced agreement would have two receivers now. And when "now" is read (P8b): "whether Ines paid the right person can change answer after she paid". | **Answered, in part**, by S15: replaced Terms stop being in force (rule 4.5); two replacements both in force give receiver now **fork** (rules 4.7, 1.4). When "now" is read: as draft 3, open (P8b). **Answered in draft 5** by S20 for the fork: the receiver now is the one named by the Terms both branches replace, shown with the fork (rules 4.7, 1.4). **Answered in draft 7** by S27 for when "now" is read: it is the version the paying or owing act names (rules 1.4, 1.8, 13.1), so whether Ines paid the right one does not change after she paid. |
| N2 | "A referrer is a seat for many; a spot holds one." The referrer spot could be filled only "for that income", which K12's inputs do not name. | **Answered** by S16: each filling is tied to its proof (rule 10.2); K12 produces fillings, each with its proof; one or many is the cMIP's. |
| N3 | "A division can take a receiver now, but not who fills a spot", and draft 3 does not say whether another layer's acts (Money's receipts and claims, and Money's verification answer) are among a K4 or K12 rule's inputs. | As draft 3. K4 still accepts receivers now and nothing like it for fillings. |
| N4 | "The thanks climbs less far than the money": no rule answers another agreement's parties now, only its receiver. | As draft 3. |
| N5 | "A relay sits on the line between holding and being seen." | **Answered** by S17: the grammar draws no such line; what proves holding is the K12 cMIP's; no use of holding is named in the grammar. Rule 11 is unchanged: a role rewarded for being seen still names its witness. |
| N6 | "Every work with a spot must name an `in_force` rule" (rule 4.2): "Not a flaw; a cost every shower work pays." | As draft 3. |
| N7 | "`receiver` is defined for being named by another agreement, not for being paid from outside." | As draft 3. **Touched in draft 7** by S23: a payer from outside pays the work, at the address its service agreement gives (rule 1.8). `receiver` itself is as draft 3. |
| M1 | Narrowed by spots, but still open: "whether an act signed by someone who is not a party is within the agreement; and whether `answers` may name an act of another agreement." | As draft 3 (F20, M1 kept). |
| M3 | Still open: "is this all the money that came in for this work?" has only the answer "all the receiver stated". | As draft 3. |
| M4 | Still open: Money gives draft 3's acts no meaning (field 5, batches, "role share"). | As draft 3. |
| M5 | Still stands: Money's answers and these do not meet. | As draft 3. |
| 8.1 | One click is the payer's, not everyone's: each receiver pays on by their own act, and nothing in draft 3 lets the payer pay every line directly and have it count. | As draft 3. **Touched in draft 7** by S23: where each work on the way points to a service agreement, one payment to the work is divided by its service, and a share owed to a work is paid to that work in the same way (rule 1.9), without each owner acting. Whether that is one click reaching everyone is for a test of cMIPs to show, not for this text to claim. |

### 9.6 For Nobody, allegedly: left open by draft 4's three changes

Nothing below is decided. Each is where test 2, or draft 4's own changes, reach past his words.

1. **When "now" is read** in rule 1.4 (P8b, test 2 N1): when the question is asked, or at the position of the act that owes. **Answered in draft 7** by S27: at neither; "now" is what the act that owes names (rule 13).
2. **Ending without replacing, and withdrawing** (P10): how Terms ended without new Terms are read, how a withdrawal of an assent or an open offer is read, and whether an open offer whose spot no one can sign for can be ended. **Answered in draft 6** by S21 and S22, except withdrawing an assent: Terms are not ended, an agreement resolves (rule 4.9); an open offer closes to new identities (rule 10.5). Withdrawing an assent: **answered in draft 8** by S34 (rule 4.10).
3. **How a fork of Terms goes on.** Rule 4.7 shows two replacements both in force and never chooses. Nothing says what, if anything, the parties can do next; `replaces` names one Terms act, so a third Terms act cannot replace both. **Answered in draft 5** by S18 to S20: the Terms both branches replace stay in force (rule 4.7); a join names every branch in `joins`, or a resolver named in advance answers (rule 12).
4. **A filling passing on, or displaced** (P7).
5. **A division reading fillings**, and other layers' acts as a rule's inputs (test 2 N3).
6. **Another agreement's parties now**, for thanks (test 2 N4).
7. **`receiver` for income from outside** (test 2 N7), **strangers' acts and `answers` across agreements** (M1), **income never stated** (M3), **Money's link to this text** (M4, M5), and **every receiver paying on** (test 2, 8.1).

### 9.7 For Nobody, allegedly: left open by draft 5's changes

Nothing below is decided. Each is where draft 5's changes reach past his words; draft 5 leaves each as draft 4 has it.

1. **A signer's fork while it stands.** S20 says what holds while a fork of Terms stands. For one signer's two stories, what the fork removes from later answers until a join or a resolver ends it is still open (P9).
2. **Two endings at once.** What is read if a join and the resolver's answer disagree, or if two joins of the same fork both carry the needed signatures (a fork of joins).
3. **Reliance that names no act.** Whether what others "relied on" covers acts that name nothing of the dropped branch: a payer in another agreement who paid the receiver now the dropped branch named, before the fork was seen (rule 1.4), or a division a rule made from it (task K4). Rule 12.5 covers only acts that name an act of the branch or are made within or under its Terms. **Answered in draft 7, in part**, by S27 and S23: a payment names the version it read (rule 13.2), so a payer who read a dropped branch has named an act of it, and what they did stands, shown as disputed (rule 12.5); an act that names nothing of the branch is not read as having relied on it (rule 13.2). A division a rule made from a dropped branch is as draft 5.
4. **The dropped branch's own signers.** What, if anything, the acts of those who signed a dropped branch keep. Draft 5 says only what rule 4.3 already says: an assent binds only while its Terms are in force.
5. **Forking, then joining, on purpose.** Whether a signer can fork and then join to take back an act they no longer like; rule 12.5 keeps only what others relied on.
6. **A join as data.** Whether the core's act format lets one act name several earlier acts, as `joins` does (compare section 8, first item).

### 9.8 For Nobody, allegedly: left open by draft 6's changes

Nothing below is decided. Each is where draft 6's changes touch something his words do not cover; draft 6 leaves each as draft 5 has it.

1. **"Still pondering."** His words on ending begin "Not sure still pondering". If his thinking moves, rule 4.9 and its readings (9.4, items 14 to 17) are what would change.
2. **A later version and a closed spot.** Whether a version after the closing one may remove a closed spot, or the fillings in it, and with whose signatures. Draft 6 says only that they stay in the closing version; rule 4.8 says who signs any later version. **Answered in draft 7** by S26: no later version changes an earned filling's rights without the holder's own signature (rule 10.6); reopening is a new version and touches no earned filling (rule 10.7).
3. **P3, a principal alone.** "Terms rule" may answer it: a grant resolves as its own Terms say, with no default for the principal. Draft 6 does not read it so, because it was said about resolving an agreement, not about grants. **Answered in draft 8** by S33: by default the delegator ends a delegation alone (rule 5.5).
4. **Withdrawing an assent** before Terms are in force (P10, F3): not covered. **Answered in draft 8** by S34 (rule 4.10).
5. **An offer whose offeror is gone** (F17): it stays open until a version closes it; if no one left can sign that version, it stays open (cost "No default way out"). Death and loss of capacity, and a pending assent, are as draft 5. **Touched in draft 7** by S25: nothing new in the grammar; the buyer's side is a contest on the record, an escrow service if one was used, and otherwise a cost.
6. **A lone sale in another agreement.** His example's cheater, selling in a separate agreement instead of within this one, is seen only as section 7 says ("Ownership is as wide as its place", F5). **Answered in draft 8** by S35: Terms for a baked work count only with the baker, or a chain from the baker (rule 14).
7. **`companion-2.md`.** Its rows for #47 Amend, #48 End and #49 Leave (an End Statement under a power; P10; "End then Divide") are not changed for draft 6. Under rule 4.9, #48 lands as a resolving version, and #49's "End then Divide" as a resolving version whose conditions divide. Whether to change the companion is for him. **Answered in draft 8** by L: `companion-3.md` is written for this text.
8. **Resolve and resolver** share a word (9.4, item 21): whether he wants another word for one of them. **Answered in draft 7** by W.

### 9.9 For Nobody, allegedly: left open by draft 7's changes

Nothing below is decided. Each is where one of draft 7's changes touches something the exchanges do not cover. Draft 7 leaves each as draft 6 has it, or says the least that lets the text be read, and says so.

1. **Receiver and service agreement together** (P8(c)). Draft 7 keeps the receiver for an agreement that points to no split. It does not say which is read when one version names both, nor whether the receiver is still wanted. **Answered in draft 8** by S28.
2. **The version a discharge divides by** (P14): the one the payment named, or the one in force when the service pays out. Draft 7 has the discharge name the version it read, and the verifier report whether it was the one in force. **Answered in draft 8** by S29.
3. **A reader's anchoring against the place the Terms name** (P13). Rule 7.2 is unchanged: where Terms name a place, only what is found there counts. **Answered in draft 8** by S30.
4. **Time for a rule** (P11): whether Terms may name the anchoring a rule reads for time, so that a `void` rule tied to time gives every reader the same answer. **Answered in draft 8** by S30: Terms may name a clock.
5. **The work's side of its deal.** A work named as a party is never bound (rule 1.2), and its part in a service agreement is its own Terms pointing to it. Not covered: whether the owners owe the fee as parties; and what an earlier service owes for payments that still arrive at its address after a new version of the work points to another service. **Answered in draft 8** by S31: the deal states both; the grammar decides neither.
6. **Rights outside fillings** (P15).
7. **What void changes besides rights.** Whether the holder of a void filling is still a party in the spot (rule 10.3), and whether a filling can leave the state void. **Answered in draft 8** by S32, in the lead's reading: as the version states.
8. **The holder's own signature, or a power** (9.4, item 30). Not answered in draft 8 (section 9.11, item 3).
9. **"Invalid" as an answer of this grammar** (9.4, item 31), and whether it should match the value layer's word (section 8; test finding M5). **Answered in draft 8** by L.
10. **Naming as a duty.** Draft 7 writes "every act names what it relied on" as a rule of reading. An act that owes and names no version is read as draft 6 read it, from the Terms in force (rule 1.4).
11. **How a payment names a version.** A payment is the value layer's act; this text can only say that a verifier reads the name (section 8). **Answered in draft 8** by L: it is Money's to say.
12. **Escrow.** Read as a service under a service agreement (9.4, item 34). Which agreement that deal is with (the offer, the buyer, both), and how a contest reaches it, are its cMIP's and untested. **Answered in draft 8** by L: they are that cMIP's.
13. **An offer that closes by itself.** The lead's proposal that an offer may name its closer or an end date was not taken up, and is not added. A `void` rule can end rights at a time; it does not close a spot to new identities. Closing stays a new version (rule 10.5). **Answered in draft 8** by S36: a spot may also close by its own signed condition (rule 10.9).
14. **One click** (test 2, 8.1): the chain is written, not tested.
15. **Where the central rule stands** (9.4, item 22): in draft 7, first in the text and tenth in number. **Answered in draft 8** by L: it is rule 1.
16. **`companion-2.md`** is not changed. Its rows that draft 7 reads differently: #13 Transfer, #26 Divide and #33 Current (which name the receiver now and rule 1.4, where draft 7 also has the work paid through its service agreement and "now" read by rule 13); #23 Hold (a service is a hand); #16 Due and #36 Wait (time per an anchoring); and #47 to #49, as draft 6 already listed. It says "rule 2 to rule 11" and "K1 to K13". Whether to write a companion for draft 7 is for him. (The rule numbers in this item are draft 8's; `companion-2.md` itself uses the old ones.) **Answered in draft 8** by L: `companion-3.md`.
17. **Words that may be typos.** He asked: "When quoting me, if you suspect a typo, run it by me pls." Four are quoted here as the ledger has them, uncorrected: "chose" (in "did they chose not to"), "split services uses" and "wether" (all in S23), and "Lasped" (S26). **Answered in draft 8** (Q2): he corrected all four on 13 November, to "choose", "service uses", "whether" and "lapsed".
18. **Still open from draft 6's list** (9.8): items 1 ("still pondering"), 3 (P3), 4 (withdrawing an assent), 6 (a lone sale in another agreement) and 7 (the companion). **In draft 8** items 3, 4, 6 and 7 are answered (S33, S34, S35, L); item 1 stays.

### 9.10 Where the ledger's arrived-at lines say more than the exchanges

The arrived-at lines are the MOR lead's, marked "to check". Each item is something a line says that the words of Nobody, allegedly, in that exchange do not themselves say, with what draft 7 does.

1. **The payment chain: "the work's side signed by its owners by weight".** His sentence is "The service has a deal with a work which happened to be signed by 3 signatures of various weights". Draft 7 reads the three signatures as the work's own (9.4, item 25) and follows the exchange: nobody signs the service agreement for the work.
2. **The payment chain: the fork.** The line says a fork of the work agreement "is then answered where the service reads the work's Terms (the last shared version while it stands); the payer never needs to know". His words in that exchange do not mention forks. Draft 7 adds no rule for it: rule 4.7 stays as draft 6 has it, and rule 1.9 has the verifier report which version a discharge read. What the payer needs to know is one address; the payment still names the version read (S27).
3. **The payment chain: "the payer pays the work".** His words do not name the payer. The sentence follows from his correction of the lead's read-back (the pointer is the work agreement's, not the payer's), and is in the brief for this draft. Written as rule 1.8.
4. **The key concept: the list.** "collectives, works, services and their deals" is the lead's list; his words are "it’s what you build with it that changes". Draft 7 uses the list as examples only, and defines none of them as a separate thing.
5. **An offer whose author is gone.** "a hand, under its service agreement", "as a cMIP" and "a stated cost" are the lead's words; his are that payment services can build on it, and that an escrow gives peace of mind for a fee. Draft 7 writes the cost because nothing in the grammar covers the loss, which is what section 7 is for, and writes no rule for escrow.
6. **A later version and a closed spot.** "A version may name the rule (a cMIP)" and "the cause shown beside it" are the lead's form for his "the agreement should have a say to give a verifier the ingredients". The last sentence of the line, on reopening a closed spot, comes after his last words in the exchange, and nothing of his answers it. Draft 7 writes it as rule 10.7 because it follows from rules 4.8 and 10.6, and marks it here to check.
7. **When "now" is read.** "every act should name what it relied on", "with no clock imposed", "and for time", and "which also covers the fork case of option A" are the lead's. His words confirm the lead's reading that an act's now is what it cites and that a place is needed only for nothing later, and add the anchoring. Draft 7 writes naming as a rule of reading (13.2), writes time into 13.4 for the reason given in rule 13's reasoning, and does not know what "option A" is (section 8).
8. **Words.** The whole line is the lead's wording, as the ledger says; row W records it as such.

### 9.11 For Nobody, allegedly: left open by draft 8's changes

Nothing below is decided. Each is where one of draft 8's changes touches something the 13 November exchanges do not cover. Draft 8 leaves each as draft 7 has it, or says the least that lets the text be read, and says so.

1. **A receiver that is an agreement, and is not the work's deal** (P8). S28 says a receiver accepts an agreement, and that for a split it is the deal with the split service. Whether a receiver may be any other agreement (one work pointing onward to another), to what depth, and what a loop answers, is as draft 7 has it: open.
2. **A payment that names no version.** S29 divides a payment by the version it was made under. A payment that names none is read as in draft 7, against the Terms in force (rule 1.9). Whether such a payment should count at all is not said.
3. **Whether S29 reaches further.** "Only a signed condition can update the agreement on one end" was said of a payment and its division. It may also answer whether a power the holder accepted can change a filling's rights (draft 7's section 9.9, item 8), and rights outside fillings (P15). Draft 8 does not read it so, and leaves both as draft 7 has them.
4. **The clock** (P16): what it is and who gives it; whether the place may be the clock; which version's clock a `void` or `closes` rule reads.
5. **"I lean a."** His answer on who may end a delegation begins "I lean". Rule 5.5 is written as the default he states. If his thinking moves, rule 5.5 is what changes.
6. **How far a signed condition on ending a delegation may go.** He chose (a), a default with conditions, over (b), a grant irrevocable by its own terms. Rule 5.5 reads no condition's content, so nothing in it stops a condition that amounts to "never". Whether that should be stopped is not said. **Answered in draft 9** by S38: it is not stopped; such a condition is not "never" but unknown, and legible (rule 5.5).
7. **A delegation from an agreement, as acts** (P17): how an act is made for an agreement, and by which act an agreement delegates or ends a delegation. Not covered; `for` and rule 1.2 are as draft 7 has them. **Touched in draft 9** by S37 (section 9.13, item 1).
8. **Void, where the version is silent.** S32 (the lead's reading) says the version states whether a void holder is still a party and whether a filling can leave void. Where it states nothing, draft 8 adds no default (9.4, item 43). **Answered in draft 9** by S39: these grammar rules define nothing more for it (rule 10.8).
9. **"A counter signature."** His words welcome one; the ledger does not record that part of the lead's proposal. Draft 8 reads it as the other parties' signatures, which put Terms in force, and writes no separate act for it. If a distinct act was meant (a signature confirming the last assent, or confirming a withdrawal), it is not in this text. **Answered in draft 9** by S37: he meant the signature that cancels the party's own earlier one. It is the act draft 8 called a withdrawal, and draft 8's reading is withdrawn (rule 4.10).
10. **Withdrawing and "every party".** Draft 8 reads "all parties have signed" as "the Terms are in force" (9.4, item 45). A pending assent whose signer has died or lost capacity (F17) is as draft 7 has it.
11. **The baker** (P18): what passes the baker's rights along the chain; several bakers; Terms that sell a work's bytes without naming its envelope; whether a licence earned by filling a spot is "an agreement for that envelope" or only rests on one; and the baker, or a holder, who sells twice. Draft 8 writes only what his three sentences say.
12. **The work baked again.** The arrived-at line says a re-baked work has "a new fingerprint whose lineage shows where it came from". Rule 14.4 says only that it is another envelope. Whether the grammar should read where it came from is not said by him; nothing in this text makes a work name what it stands on (section 0: "Mor won’t force the shower").
13. **Closing by itself.** A spot that closed under its own condition is reopened only by a new version (rule 10.7). Whether a `closes` rule may itself reopen a spot, and what "first 100 buyers" means where the agreement names no place, are not said; without a place the count is only as shown.
14. **A silent deal** (rule 1.7): where a deal states nothing on the fee or on late payments, the answer is not shown, and the cost is stated (section 7).
15. **"Invalid" and "not shown"** (P19): the line between them is drawn by Claude, and the other layers were not read.
16. **Nothing was tested.** No test was run against draft 8. Whether one click reaches every contributor (test 2, 8.1) is still for a test of cMIPs to show.
17. **Still open from draft 7's list** (9.9): items 6 (rights outside fillings, P15), 8 (the holder's own signature, or a power), 10 (naming as a duty) and 14 (one click); and, from draft 6's list (9.8), item 1 ("still pondering").

### 9.12 Where the ledger's arrived-at lines for 13 November say more than the exchanges

The arrived-at lines are the MOR lead's, marked "to check". Each item is something a line says that the words of Nobody, allegedly, in that exchange do not themselves say, with what draft 8 does.

1. **The receiver: "one mechanism, not two".** His words are that the receiver still exists and also accepts agreement hashes. That the field `service` goes is the lead's consequence. Draft 8 follows it, because his words answer the lead's proposal to keep both, and marks it to check (9.4, item 36). The parenthesis "each work still has its own signed deal" is the lead's: in draft 8, as in draft 7, nobody signs the deal for the work; the work's part is its own Terms naming the deal (rule 1.6).
2. **The discharge: "so Ana gets 50% of Fen's payment", and "the one the payer accepted and its act names".** Both are the lead's, drawn from his case. His words are "Whatever was signed (I accept your offer) matters". Draft 8 follows the line: the version a payment was made under is the one the payment names (rule 1.9).
3. **Whose anchoring wins: "the place and the clock".** His sentence names the reference clock only. The place, and "a reader's own anchoring applies only where the agreement names none", are in the lead's proposal that his sentence answers. Draft 8 writes both (rules 7.4, 13.4), reading the exchange as a whole.
4. **What void changes.** The whole entry is the lead's reading. It records no words of his about void: his sentence, "The agreement states all that is needed to solve these", was said of the fee and of late payments (S31). Draft 8 writes it, as its brief asks, and marks it here as the one change of draft 8 that rests on no answer of his to its own question. **Answered in draft 9** by S39: asked directly, he answered, and then said that "void is the wrong term". Rule 10.8 is rewritten from his answer and the lead's wording (W2).
5. **Grants.** "by its own signature (an identity) or by its Terms' rule (an agreement)", and the examples "(notice, a term, a cause)" and "(pay, notice, severance)", are the lead's. His words are "The default is delegator can end it, but with provision that an agreement can add conditions to its ability to do so", and that a cMIP should be able to defend employment rights if the parties need it. Draft 8 uses the examples as examples and says, for a delegator that is an agreement, only "what that agreement's own Terms say".
6. **Withdrawing an assent.** "by a signed act" is the lead's. "every party" is his "all parties"; draft 8 reads it as "in force" (9.4, item 45). "a counter signature" is in his words and not in the line (section 9.11, item 9). **Answered in draft 9** by S37, and by the line the ledger now adds.
7. **A lone sale.** In the first line, "(and those holding from them)" comes before his words on the chain, and "which gives a new fingerprint whose lineage shows where it came from" is the lead's. Draft 8 writes the holders only through the chain he then confirmed (rule 14.2(b)), and writes nothing on lineage (section 9.11, item 12). The second line follows his words.
8. **An offer that closes by itself.** The proposal and its two examples are the lead's; his words are "Yes. And agreed". Draft 8 writes the proposal as rule 10.9.
9. **The rest of draft 7's list.** The whole entry is the lead's settlement, as the ledger says; row L records it as such.

### 9.13 For Nobody, allegedly: left open by draft 9's changes

Nothing below is decided. Each is where one of draft 9's changes touches something the exchanges do not cover. Draft 9 leaves each as draft 8 has it, or says the least that lets the text be read, and says so.

1. **An agreement that "signs".** In plain words: his answer on the counter signature speaks of an agreement as a party having signed ("all signature or whatever satisfies the collective agreement"). Draft 8's rules 1.2 and 4.4 say the opposite: a party named as an agreement never assents and is never counted when Terms come into force. The two do not meet, and the exchange was not about that. Draft 9 writes only how "signed" is read for such a party (rule 4.10), and leaves rules 1.2 and 4.4, and the field `for`, as draft 8 has them. Not said: whether an agreement named as a party must sign before Terms are in force; by which acts of its own signers that is shown; and whether such a party can itself make a counter signature (P17). This is the one place where draft 9 found his words and the rules pulling apart, and it is his to settle.
2. **"Every party" and an `in_force` rule.** Draft 9 reads "once all parties have signed" as "once the Terms are in force", as draft 8 did (9.4, items 45 and 51). Where Terms come into force on fewer signatures than everyone's, a counter signature is then unavailable before every party has signed. If he meant every party, whatever the Terms say, rule 4.10 changes.
3. **A counter signature made by someone else.** His words say the signature cancels "the previous one"; the lead's line says the party's "own". Rule 4.10 is written for the signer of the assent. Whether an agent may counter-sign for the one it acts for, or a principal cancel an assent its agent gave, is not said.
4. **A pending assent whose signer is gone** (F17): as draft 8 has it. A counter signature needs its signer.
5. **"No limit", elsewhere.** S38 was asked and answered about conditions on ending a delegation. Draft 9 writes "no limit" there only (rule 5.5). Whether it also holds for the other signed conditions this text reads (on a payment's division, rule 1.9; on rights, rule 10.8; on closing, rule 10.9) is not said; nothing in draft 8 limits those either.
6. **Coming back by condition.** His words: "it is updating the agreement within its conditions. Nothing stops the agreement to be updated again." The lead's line, and this draft's brief, read going on as a new version. His sentence could also mean a second change within the same signed conditions, with no new version (rights that stop when a fee is unpaid and apply again when it is paid). Rule 10.8 writes only the new version. It does not forbid a condition whose rule gives a different answer once later acts are held; it does not say so either.
7. **Reused values, and the version a filling was earned under.** Rule 10.6 says a filling is earned under the version its proof names. A renewed offer that plugs in values already in the chain rests on acts that name the earlier version. How a filling resting on them is read as earned under the renewed version is not said; rule 10.8 leaves it to that version's K12 cMIP, and rule 10.6 is as draft 8 has it.
8. **Who signs a renewed offer.** "Nothing stops the agreement to be updated again" is written as: a later version MAY offer again, made as rule 4.8 says. Under rule 4.8 as draft 8 has it, those already in the spot and bound by their own assents sign too, unless the Terms give that power to fewer (9.4, item 20). So something can stop it: a signature withheld. His words do not say whether that is meant.
9. **The holder, where the version is silent.** Whether the holder of a filling no longer in force is still a party "depends on how things are setup". Where the version sets nothing up, rule 10.3 reads as it did: a party from then on. No default is added.
10. **One condition, or two fields.** His words speak of the agreement "updated within its specified conditions (some updates stop the offer, some don’t)": one notion, a condition, which may or may not stop the offer. Draft 9 keeps draft 8's two fields: `condition` on a version, for rights, and `closes` on a role, for the offer. Whether they should be one is not said (also section 9.15, item 5).
11. **"In force", twice.** The lead's wording uses "in force" of a right or a filling. Rule 4 uses it of Terms. Draft 9 defines both under one word (section 2), as the wording asks. They are answered differently: Terms in force is an answer about what is latest (rule 4.6); a filling in force is what its own condition says (rule 10.8). If one word for both misleads, the wording is the lead's to change.
12. **The names.** `condition` for the field and `counter_signature` for the kind are Claude's; "no longer in force" is the lead's wording used as a verifier's answer.
13. **Words that may be typos.** He asked: "When quoting me, if you suspect a typo, run it by me pls." Three are quoted here as the ledger has them, uncorrected: "don.t" (in S38), and "watever" and "techincally" (in S39).
14. **Not brought in.** The ledger's "13 November" also records an aside of his, on a test in which the grant was refused. It is not among the four things this draft was asked to bring in, and nothing is written from it. It may bear on P17.
15. **The delegator who signs away ending.** With no limit on conditions, a delegation only the agent may end holds the delegator for as long as the agent chooses, and if the agent is gone, for good (costs "An agent has what was signed, and no more" and "No default way out"). Written as a cost, not closed.
16. **Nothing was tested.** No test was run against draft 9. Whether one click reaches every contributor (test 2, 8.1) is still for a test of cMIPs to show.
17. **Still open from draft 8's list** (9.11): items 1 to 5, 7 (touched), and 10 to 17.

### 9.14 Where the ledger's arrived-at lines for draft 9's exchanges say more than the exchanges

The arrived-at lines are the MOR lead's, marked "to check". Each item is something a line says that the words of Nobody, allegedly, in that exchange do not themselves say, with what draft 9 does.

1. **Withdrawing an assent.** The line follows his words closely. "its own earlier assent" is the lead's for his "the previous one"; "(all its signers, or what its rule accepts)" is the lead's for his "all signature or whatever satisfies the collective agreement"; "that agreement's own Terms" for "the collective agreement". Draft 9 follows the line. The line says "signed" means that for an agreement as a party without saying where that meets the rules under which such a party never signs; neither do his words (section 9.13, item 1).
2. **Grants.** "no limit on what a signed condition may say" is wider than his words, which answer the one case put to him: "it’s a I don.t know, not a never". The question offered that case as "the one limit on conditions", so the exchange as a whole supports no limit on conditions on ending a delegation, and draft 9 writes it for those only (section 9.13, item 5). "a legible choice the delegator signed" is the lead's; draft 9 writes "legible" as: the condition is read from the signed Terms and shown.
3. **What void changes, the first line.** "nothing more is defined for it" is the lead's; his words are "The question depends on how things are setup". Draft 9 writes both: nothing more is defined, and what follows is as the version states. "the agreement can always be given a new version again" says more than the exchange: his words are "Nothing stops the agreement to be updated again", which name no version and do not say "always". Draft 9 follows the exchange: a later version MAY offer again, made as rule 4.8 says, so with the signatures the Terms require (section 9.13, items 6 and 8). "an offer renewed" is the lead's for his "offering again".
4. **What void changes, the lead's wording.** The whole of it is the lead's, as the ledger says; row W2 records it as such. His own words are "updated within its specified conditions (some updates stop the offer, some don’t)". "Some conditions close an offer, some do not" puts "conditions" where he says "updates"; "the condition named beside it" continues draft 7's "the cause shown beside it".
5. **The principle.** The ledger records "Building blocks over solutions." as a principle of his, with no exchange and no line. That it is "the test for every change", and that it is applied to draft 8's text as a list, are this draft's brief, not the ledger.

### 9.15 Building blocks over solutions: where the text may solve what a cMIP could build

For Nobody, allegedly. B is the test for every change from now on. This list applies it backwards, to what drafts 1 to 8 wrote, and is Claude's reading only. Each item is something in this text that looks like a solution, and that a cMIP could perhaps build from the grammar's blocks. **Nothing here is removed or changed in draft 9.** Most items come from his own words; they are listed so that he can say which are blocks after all.

1. **The payment chain** (rules 1.6 to 1.9). The text itself calls it "the first thing built with it". The blocks under it: an agreement as a party (1.1); a receiver that may be an agreement (1.3); an act naming the version it read (rule 13.2); saying who said it (rule 8); tasks K1, K4 and K8. What reads like a solution: the test for what counts as a deal (1.6), the list of what is read from a service agreement (address, fee, late payments, 1.7), the four things read to say a work was paid (1.8), and the matching of a discharge (1.9). From S23, S28, S29 and S31.
2. **Seen, by the agreed witness** (rule 11). A role rewarded for being seen could be a spot (rule 10) whose cMIP names a witness (task K5) and a rule (task K4); the field `witness` and clauses 11.1 to 11.5 would then be that cMIP's. The field `settlement` is a socket for Money's proof, which he confirmed, and reads as a block. From S9 and S12 to S14.
3. **Four named kinds of spot** (rule 10.1). The fifth kind, future, is the general block: its cMIP says who the participant is and how they are confirmed. Done, received, holding and fits are narrower cases a cMIP could state. They are his own four (S7); the text keeps them because a kind tells the reader whom an answer rests on.
4. **Closing, reopening and closing by itself** (rules 10.5, 10.7, 10.9; `closed`, `closes`). Whether a spot still takes new identities could be what its K12 cMIP computes from the version in force. From S22, S26 (10.7 is the lead's) and S36.
5. **Two fields for one notion** (`condition`, rule 10.8; `closes`, rule 10.9). After S39 both are a condition signed in advance, one about rights and one about the offer. One block, a condition that itself says whether it stops the offer, might do for both (section 9.13, item 10).
6. **The baker's chain** (rules 14.2 and 14.3). The blocks: Terms naming an envelope (14.1), acts naming what they relied on (rule 13.2), and a spot of kind received for whoever holds from the baker (rule 10). The test "counts only if" might be a cMIP's. From S35, his three sentences.
7. **The delegator's default** (rule 5.5). A delegation's own Terms could give the power to replace them to the delegator alone (rules 5.2, 4.8); the default saves writing it. From S33: "The default is delegator can end it".
8. **The counter signature** (rule 4.10). Where Terms carry their own `in_force` rule, that rule (task K4) could leave out an assent followed by a Statement of a kind a cMIP defines (task K11). For Terms with no `in_force` rule there is no cMIP to do it, so there the grammar must read it. From S34 and S37.
9. **The arbiter** (rule 12.4, task K14, `arbiter`). A fork could perhaps be ended by a power (rule 5), given in advance, to sign the join. From S19.
10. **Read as blocks, and not listed above:** the agreement as a party, the receiver, spots as such, `relies_on`, the place and the clock, the answers, and the tasks.

**Kept as "chain", on purpose.** His quoted words, everywhere ("values already in the chain", "chains crossing, knots forming", "the chain of agreements of the work", "the whole chain of payment and also the chain of agreements that dictates"), and the research's ("by forging the chain"). ## C.4 Where the ledger's arrived-at lines for draft 10's exchanges say more than the exchanges

The arrived-at lines are the MOR lead's, marked "to check". Each item is something a line says that the exchange, read as a whole, does not itself say, with what draft 10 does.

1. **"the split stands as proposed"** (Building blocks). His words are "Feels right. Any doubts in your end?": an assent, followed at once by a question that reopened it. The lead's doubt and his answer then changed the proposal (the shared shape). Draft 10 follows the exchange: the split as proposed, with the shape added.
2. **"the choice of cMIP is per leg, never for the whole chain."** His words: "does not require to always use the same cMIPs, the choice of cMIP stands on the leg, not the whole". "Never" is the lead's. Draft 10 writes: nothing is chosen for a sequence as a whole, and no leg's choice binds another; legs may name the same cMIPs, and none is required to (rule 11.1).
3. **"each leg reports its result in one shared shape."** His words: "informing everyone of the whole where needed has its value". "Where needed" is his and is not in the line; "each leg reports" is the lead's. Draft 10 follows the exchange: rule 11 defines the report and makes no leg give one; where one is missing, the rest of the sequence is not shown (rule 11.4; draft 10, section 8.2, item 4).
4. **"(received, passed on, to whom)."** The three parts are the lead's, from his own proposal as the ledger gives it ("this agreement received X and passed on these shares"); "to whom" is added in the line. His answer agrees with the proposal ("I think you are right") without restating the parts. Draft 10 writes the three parts, and lists what they leave unsaid (P20).
5. **"where a result depends on a chain of agreements."** His words name two things: "the whole chain of payment and also the chain of agreements that dictates". The line, and the brief, read them as any sequence of agreements. Draft 10 writes rule 11 for any sequence, and leaves the baker's (rule 14) as draft 9 has it (P18).
6. **"'chain' names only the versions of one act (one agreement's own history)"** (Chain and sequence). His words: "chain only points at chains of the same act… if that makes any sense". "The versions" and "one agreement's own history" are the lead's gloss on "the same act", and he ended on a doubt. Draft 10 defines chain as the versions of one agreement. Uses of "chain" for something that is neither (one signer's own acts) are not covered by the exchange, and are left (C.3).
7. **"(a payment passing from one to the next, the baker's rights passing on)."** The two examples are the lead's. His words name only "chain of agreements"; his own earlier words on the baker were "through the chain of agreements of the work", so the baker's is a sequence on his own words.
8. **"The MOR lead applies this throughout draft 10 as technical wording."** The lead's own statement, recorded as the lead's.

# Part D. Draft 11

**Read for draft 11, and nothing else:** `docs/agreements-from-scratch/draft-10.md`, this file and `companion-3.md` (branch `claude/agreements-draft-10`, pull request 162); `docs/decisions.md` and `docs/lead/proposals/report-block-1.md` (branch `claude/lead-records-2`); and `docs/click-sketch-report.md` (branch `claude/click-sketch`, pull request 163: the Dubsar Suite lead's test of draft 10). Draft 11 is draft 10 with five changes, numbered as its brief numbers them, and nothing else. Three exchanges of the ledger are brought in, each read as a whole: **"Must every leg report?"** (S42), **"The report, after the click test"** (S43) and **"May and can't"** (S44). `report-block-1.md` is the MOR lead's proposal from those exchanges, not a decision; draft 11 uses the report as it describes it, and does not define it. The fifth change is housekeeping (H2). No research text, earlier draft or sketch code was reopened.

## D.1 Changes, and the words each comes from

| | The exchange | What changed (Claude's reading) |
|---|---|---|
| S42 | **Must every leg report?** (draft 10, its section 8.2, item 4). The MOR lead proposed keeping his "where needed": the report defined, no leg made to give one. He: "When needed could be all the time that we did not check". The MOR lead then proposed leaving rule 11 as draft 10 has it and letting the shower test answer it. He: "Good idea, test by building on top, I like it" | Nothing by itself: the exchange decides nothing and sends the question to a test. The test was built (D.2). Its answer, and the next exchange, are what change rule 11. Both sentences of his are quoted in rule 11's reasoning. |
| S43 | **The report, after the click test** (pull request 163). Asked what is truly useful in a leg report, the MOR lead proposing the settlement as the link between two reports. He: "We need to be able to track it both directions, as exercise. The agreements need to point to the right places to receive the confirmations." To a field "where my reports are found": "feels too broad", and: "Tasks may or may not call for a report. Therefore, a task can if needed specify where it expect its report from." Asked whether that is a rule for all of MOR: "Yes, I lean so too. It is elegant, and can be used wherever it is needed. The real question is: is this task unidirectional or bidirectional?" Asked whether a report carries only what it answers, what it relied on and who signs: "Seems enough yes".
